It’s not that I want to keep going over it… I just don’t always seem to be able to let it go. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mainly connected to your retirement and handing over the practice?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… or at least that’s where it tends to start. The retirement and the handover are sort of the big themes.\n\nBut it doesn’t stay neatly in one box, if that makes sense. My mind keeps circling—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the financial side of the practice handoff is really sound, whether I’m going to end up feeling… well, a bit useless once I’m fully retired. I know that may sound excessive, and part of me can see it’s probably out of proportion. I just don’t always manage to make it feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel like they would mostly go away if the retirement and practice handoff were settled?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, because that would make it sound very tidy, wouldn’t it? And I do think settling the handoff would help.\n\nBut honestly… I’m not sure it would all just disappear. My mind tends to keep circling around things—the old patients, whether they’re being properly looked after, the finances of the handoff, and then this worry about whether I’ll be of any use once I’m fully retired. Even to me, it can feel a bit out of proportion. I know that, but knowing it doesn’t always stop it.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from retirement and handing over the practice, do you often find yourself worrying a lot about other parts of life too?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Or even when I’m trying to do a watercolor wash, my mind just wanders off to everything that could go wrong. \n\nI don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should ask someone.\n\nDoctor: When your concentration slips, is it usually because you’re worrying about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair. I wouldn’t want to overstate it, but a lot of the time when I lose the thread, it’s because my mind has wandered into worry.\n\nIt sort of circles around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the handoff of the practice is really financially sound, and… I suppose whether I’ll end up feeling useless once I’m fully retired. Even saying that, it sounds a bit dramatic, doesn’t it? But yes, the amount I worry does feel out of proportion sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you find it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than I’d like to admit, probably. I do try to talk myself through it, you know, reason with myself the way I would with a nervous patient and say, “All right, let’s just look at what’s actually in front of us.”\n\nBut often one concern just sort of leads into the next. Before I know it, I’m checking emails again, or looking back through notes, or asking the same question I’ve already asked, just to reassure myself. It’s not that I want to keep going over it… I just don’t always seem to be able to let it go. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mainly connected to your retirement and handing over the practice?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… or at least that’s where it tends to start. The retirement and the handover are sort of the big themes.\n\nBut it doesn’t stay neatly in one box, if that makes sense. My mind keeps circling—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the financial side of the practice handoff is really sound, whether I’m going to end up feeling… well, a bit useless once I’m fully retired. I know that may sound excessive, and part of me can see it’s probably out of proportion. I just don’t always manage to make it feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel like they would mostly go away if the retirement and practice handoff were settled?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, because that would make it sound very tidy, wouldn’t it? And I do think settling the handoff would help.\n\nBut honestly… I’m not sure it would all just disappear. My mind tends to keep circling around things—the old patients, whether they’re being properly looked after, the finances of the handoff, and then this worry about whether I’ll be of any use once I’m fully retired. Even to me, it can feel a bit out of proportion. I know that, but knowing it doesn’t always stop it.\n\nDoctor: Aside from retirement and handing over the practice, do you often find yourself worrying a lot about other parts of life too?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt tends to start with the practice or retirement, but then my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the handoff is financially sound, whether I’ll become… useless, I suppose, once I’m fully retired. And even as I’m saying it, I can hear that it’s probably out of proportion.\n\nI’m not sure if that answers what you’re asking, but yes, the worry does seem to spread beyond one neat issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single day, but more days than not, yes.\n\nIt really started when I began the formal retirement process, which was about… a year and three months ago now. I suppose I kept telling myself it was just a normal reaction to a big change, but it has been going on that long. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. I mean… I don’t think I’m pacing the room or anything like that.\n\nThere are times I feel a bit keyed up, I suppose, like I can’t quite settle, but I’ve tended to think of that as just being busy-minded rather than anything dramatic. Is that the kind of thing you mean by restless or on edge?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Or even when I’m trying to do a watercolor wash, my mind just wanders off to everything that could go wrong. \n\nI don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should ask someone.\n\nDoctor: When your concentration slips, is it usually because you’re worrying about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair. I wouldn’t want to overstate it, but a lot of the time when I lose the thread, it’s because my mind has wandered into worry.\n\nIt sort of circles around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the handoff of the practice is really financially sound, and… I suppose whether I’ll end up feeling useless once I’m fully retired. Even saying that, it sounds a bit dramatic, doesn’t it? But yes, the amount I worry does feel out of proportion sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you find it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than I’d like to admit, probably. I do try to talk myself through it, you know, reason with myself the way I would with a nervous patient and say, “All right, let’s just look at what’s actually in front of us.”\n\nBut often one concern just sort of leads into the next. It’s probably not disastrous, but at the clinic I’ll be reviewing a chart and suddenly realize I’ve read the same line three times. Or even when I’m trying to do a watercolor wash, my mind just wanders off to everything that could go wrong. \n\nI don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should ask someone.\n\nDoctor: When your concentration slips, is it usually because you’re worrying about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair. I wouldn’t want to overstate it, but a lot of the time when I lose the thread, it’s because my mind has wandered into worry.\n\nIt sort of circles around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the handoff of the practice is really financially sound, and… I suppose whether I’ll end up feeling useless once I’m fully retired. Even saying that, it sounds a bit dramatic, doesn’t it? But yes, the amount I worry does feel out of proportion sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you find it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than I’d like to admit, probably. I do try to talk myself through it, you know, reason with myself the way I would with a nervous patient and say, “All right, let’s just look at what’s actually in front of us.”\n\nBut often one concern just sort of leads into the next. Before I know it, I’m checking emails again, or looking back through notes, or asking the same question I’ve already asked, just to reassure myself. It’s not that I want to keep going over it… I just don’t always seem to be able to let it go. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mainly connected to your retirement and handing over the practice?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… or at least that’s where it tends to start. The retirement and the handover are sort of the big themes.\n\nBut it doesn’t stay neatly in one box, if that makes sense. My mind keeps circling—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the financial side of the practice handoff is really sound, whether I’m going to end up feeling… well, a bit useless once I’m fully retired. I know that may sound excessive, and part of me can see it’s probably out of proportion. I just don’t always manage to make it feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel like they would mostly go away if the retirement and practice handoff were settled?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, because that would make it sound very tidy, wouldn’t it? I do try to talk myself through it, you know, reason with myself the way I would with a nervous patient and say, “All right, let’s just look at what’s actually in front of us.”\n\nBut often one concern just sort of leads into the next. Before I know it, I’m checking emails again, or looking back through notes, or asking the same question I’ve already asked, just to reassure myself. It’s not that I want to keep going over it… I just don’t always seem to be able to let it go. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mainly connected to your retirement and handing over the practice?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… or at least that’s where it tends to start. The retirement and the handover are sort of the big themes.\n\nBut it doesn’t stay neatly in one box, if that makes sense. My mind keeps circling—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the financial side of the practice handoff is really sound, whether I’m going to end up feeling… well, a bit useless once I’m fully retired. I know that may sound excessive, and part of me can see it’s probably out of proportion. I just don’t always manage to make it feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel like they would mostly go away if the retirement and practice handoff were settled?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, because that would make it sound very tidy, wouldn’t it? And I do think settling the handoff would help.\n\nBut honestly… I’m not sure it would all just disappear. My mind tends to keep circling around things—the old patients, whether they’re being properly looked after, the finances of the handoff, and then this worry about whether I’ll be of any use once I’m fully retired. Even to me, it can feel a bit out of proportion. I know that, but knowing it doesn’t always stop it.\n\nDoctor: Aside from retirement and handing over the practice, do you often find yourself worrying a lot about other parts of life too?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt tends to start with the practice or retirement, but then my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the handoff is financially sound, whether I’ll become… useless, I suppose, once I’m fully retired. And even as I’m saying it, I can hear that it’s probably out of proportion.\n\nI’m not sure if that answers what you’re asking, but yes, the worry does seem to spread beyond one neat issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single day, but more days than not, yes.\n\nIt really started when I began the formal retirement process, which was about… a year and three months ago now. I suppose I kept telling myself it was just a normal reaction to a big change, but it has been going on that long. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. I mean… I don’t think I’m pacing the room or anything like that.\n\nThere are times I feel a bit keyed up, I suppose, like I can’t quite settle, but I’ve tended to think of that as just being busy-minded rather than anything dramatic. Is that the kind of thing you mean by restless or on edge?\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense?\n\nPatient: Not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I’m 64, so I get the usual stiff neck or shoulders now and then, but I haven’t really thought of it as “muscle tension” in that sense.\n\nMaybe I should pay more attention to it, but no… I wouldn’t say it’s something I often notice. Is that an acceptable answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have low moments like anyone, and I can get a bit discouraged, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been depressed much of the time.\n\nAt least… that’s how it feels to me. Is that clear enough?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired or fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit maybe.\n\nBy midafternoon I can feel pretty worn out, and it’s not always fixed by having a quiet morning or even resting a bit. Little things, like weeding the garden or standing at the easel, can feel heavier than they used to. I’d been telling myself that’s just age or being busy, but… yes, I do notice it. Is that the sort of fatigue you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but most nights I’ll get into bed tired, and then once the house is quiet my mind starts going over things — patient transfers, money details, that sort of thing.\n\nI do sleep some, but I’m often awake around four or five, and it feels like I never really settled properly. Does that count as trouble sleeping?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I wouldn’t want to overstate it, but a lot of the time when I lose the thread, it’s because my mind has wandered into worry.\n\nIt sort of circles around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the handoff of the practice is really financially sound, and… I suppose whether I’ll end up feeling useless once I’m fully retired. Even saying that, it sounds a bit dramatic, doesn’t it? But yes, the amount I worry does feel out of proportion sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you find it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than I’d like to admit, probably. I do try to talk myself through it, you know, reason with myself the way I would with a nervous patient and say, “All right, let’s just look at what’s actually in front of us.”\n\nBut often one concern just sort of leads into the next. Before I know it, I’m checking emails again, or looking back through notes, or asking the same question I’ve already asked, just to reassure myself. It’s not that I want to keep going over it… I just don’t always seem to be able to let it go. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mainly connected to your retirement and handing over the practice?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… or at least that’s where it tends to start. The retirement and the handover are sort of the big themes.\n\nBut it doesn’t stay neatly in one box, if that makes sense. My mind keeps circling—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the financial side of the practice handoff is really sound, whether I’m going to end up feeling… well, a bit useless once I’m fully retired. I know that may sound excessive, and part of me can see it’s probably out of proportion. I just don’t always manage to make it feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel like they would mostly go away if the retirement and practice handoff were settled?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, because that would make it sound very tidy, wouldn’t it? And I do think settling the handoff would help.\n\nBut honestly… I’m not sure it would all just disappear. My mind tends to keep circling around things—the old patients, whether they’re being properly looked after, the finances of the handoff, and then this worry about whether I’ll be of any use once I’m fully retired. Even to me, it can feel a bit out of proportion. I know that, but knowing it doesn’t always stop it.\n\nDoctor: Aside from retirement and handing over the practice, do you often find yourself worrying a lot about other parts of life too?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt tends to start with the practice or retirement, but then my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the handoff is financially sound, whether I’ll become… useless, I suppose, once I’m fully retired. And even as I’m saying it, I can hear that it’s probably out of proportion.\n\nI’m not sure if that answers what you’re asking, but yes, the worry does seem to spread beyond one neat issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single day, but more days than not, yes.\n\nIt really started when I began the formal retirement process, which was about… a year and three months ago now. I suppose I kept telling myself it was just a normal reaction to a big change, but it has been going on that long. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. I mean… I don’t think I’m pacing the room or anything like that.\n\nThere are times I feel a bit keyed up, I suppose, like I can’t quite settle, but I’ve tended to think of that as just being busy-minded rather than anything dramatic. Is that the kind of thing you mean by restless or on edge?\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense?\n\nPatient: Not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I’m 64, so I get the usual stiff neck or shoulders now and then, but I haven’t really thought of it as “muscle tension” in that sense.\n\nMaybe I should pay more attention to it, but no… I wouldn’t say it’s something I often notice. Is that an acceptable answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have low moments like anyone, and I can get a bit discouraged, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been depressed much of the time.\n\nAt least… that’s how it feels to me. Is that clear enough?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired or fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit maybe.\n\nBy midafternoon I can feel pretty worn out, and it’s not always fixed by having a quiet morning or even resting a bit. Little things, like weeding the garden or standing at the easel, can feel heavier than they used to. I’d been telling myself that’s just age or being busy, but… yes, I do notice it. Is that the sort of fatigue you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but most nights I’ll get into bed tired, and then once the house is quiet my mind starts going over things — patient transfers, money details, that sort of thing.\n\nI do sleep some, but I’m often awake around four or five, and it feels like I never really settled properly. Does that count as trouble sleeping?\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they have, though I’ve been trying not to let them.\n\nThe retirement handoff at the clinic has slowed down more than it probably should. I keep second-guessing decisions and then lose time going back over things, so I’ve had to postpone bits of it. And at home, even the painting and gardening don’t feel quite as manageable as they used to.\n\nI’m still functioning, I suppose, but it’s definitely getting in the way. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I still like the idea of painting and being in the garden, and when I do get myself into it, there can still be some pleasure there.\n\nMaybe I don’t reach for those things quite as easily as I used to, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It feels more like… I’m a bit worn down, rather than not caring about them. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I can get frustrated with myself when I’m not keeping up the way I think I should, but I wouldn’t say I feel worthless.\n\nGuilty… maybe in the ordinary sense, like feeling I should be handling things better. But not some deep guilt, if that’s what you mean. Is that an important distinction?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what finally got me to make the appointment is that my concentration hasn’t been as reliable as I’d like. It’s probably not disastrous, but at the clinic I’ll be reviewing a chart and suddenly realize I’ve read the same line three times. Or even when I’m trying to do a watercolor wash, my mind just wanders off to everything that could go wrong. \n\nI don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should ask someone.\n\nDoctor: When your concentration slips, is it usually because you’re worrying about things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair. I wouldn’t want to overstate it, but a lot of the time when I lose the thread, it’s because my mind has wandered into worry.\n\nIt sort of circles around things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the handoff of the practice is really financially sound, and… I suppose whether I’ll end up feeling useless once I’m fully retired. Even saying that, it sounds a bit dramatic, doesn’t it? But yes, the amount I worry does feel out of proportion sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, do you find it hard to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than I’d like to admit, probably. I do try to talk myself through it, you know, reason with myself the way I would with a nervous patient and say, “All right, let’s just look at what’s actually in front of us.”\n\nBut often one concern just sort of leads into the next. Before I know it, I’m checking emails again, or looking back through notes, or asking the same question I’ve already asked, just to reassure myself. It’s not that I want to keep going over it… I just don’t always seem to be able to let it go. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel mainly connected to your retirement and handing over the practice?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… or at least that’s where it tends to start. The retirement and the handover are sort of the big themes.\n\nBut it doesn’t stay neatly in one box, if that makes sense. My mind keeps circling—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the financial side of the practice handoff is really sound, whether I’m going to end up feeling… well, a bit useless once I’m fully retired. I know that may sound excessive, and part of me can see it’s probably out of proportion. I just don’t always manage to make it feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do these worries feel like they would mostly go away if the retirement and practice handoff were settled?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes, because that would make it sound very tidy, wouldn’t it? And I do think settling the handoff would help.\n\nBut honestly… I’m not sure it would all just disappear. My mind tends to keep circling around things—the old patients, whether they’re being properly looked after, the finances of the handoff, and then this worry about whether I’ll be of any use once I’m fully retired. Even to me, it can feel a bit out of proportion. I know that, but knowing it doesn’t always stop it.\n\nDoctor: Aside from retirement and handing over the practice, do you often find yourself worrying a lot about other parts of life too?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt tends to start with the practice or retirement, but then my mind keeps circling around all sorts of things—whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the handoff is financially sound, whether I’ll become… useless, I suppose, once I’m fully retired. And even as I’m saying it, I can hear that it’s probably out of proportion.\n\nI’m not sure if that answers what you’re asking, but yes, the worry does seem to spread beyond one neat issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying like this more days than not for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single day, but more days than not, yes.\n\nIt really started when I began the formal retirement process, which was about… a year and three months ago now. I suppose I kept telling myself it was just a normal reaction to a big change, but it has been going on that long. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. I mean… I don’t think I’m pacing the room or anything like that.\n\nThere are times I feel a bit keyed up, I suppose, like I can’t quite settle, but I’ve tended to think of that as just being busy-minded rather than anything dramatic. Is that the kind of thing you mean by restless or on edge?\n\nDoctor: Do you often notice your muscles feeling tense?\n\nPatient: Not especially, I don’t think. I mean, I’m 64, so I get the usual stiff neck or shoulders now and then, but I haven’t really thought of it as “muscle tension” in that sense.\n\nMaybe I should pay more attention to it, but no… I wouldn’t say it’s something I often notice. Is that an acceptable answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I have low moments like anyone, and I can get a bit discouraged, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been depressed much of the time.\n\nAt least… that’s how it feels to me. Is that clear enough?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel easily tired or fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit maybe.\n\nBy midafternoon I can feel pretty worn out, and it’s not always fixed by having a quiet morning or even resting a bit. Little things, like weeding the garden or standing at the easel, can feel heavier than they used to. I’d been telling myself that’s just age or being busy, but… yes, I do notice it. Is that the sort of fatigue you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes… somewhat. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but most nights I’ll get into bed tired, and then once the house is quiet my mind starts going over things — patient transfers, money details, that sort of thing.\n\nI do sleep some, but I’m often awake around four or five, and it feels like I never really settled properly. Does that count as trouble sleeping?\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they have, though I’ve been trying not to let them.\n\nThe retirement handoff at the clinic has slowed down more than it probably should. I keep second-guessing decisions and then lose time going back over things, so I’ve had to postpone bits of it. And at home, even the painting and gardening don’t feel quite as manageable as they used to.\n\nI’m still functioning, I suppose, but it’s definitely getting in the way. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I still like the idea of painting and being in the garden, and when I do get myself into it, there can still be some pleasure there.\n\nMaybe I don’t reach for those things quite as easily as I used to, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It feels more like… I’m a bit worn down, rather than not caring about them. Does that distinction make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I can get frustrated with myself when I’m not keeping up the way I think I should, but I wouldn’t say I feel worthless.\n\nGuilty… maybe in the ordinary sense, like feeling I should be handling things better. But not some deep guilt, if that’s what you mean. Is that an important distinction?\n\nDoctor: Outside of the retirement and practice handoff, do you find yourself worrying a lot about other everyday things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. I try to tell myself it’s all just part of the transition, but my mind doesn’t seem to stay neatly on that one subject.\n\nI’ll worry about whether my old patients are being looked after properly, or whether the financial side of the handoff is really sound. And then it sort of turns into bigger things, like whether I’ll become… I don’t know, useless, once I’m fully retired.\n\nEven saying that sounds a bit dramatic, and I know some of it is probably out of proportion. But yes, it does circle around more everyday things than I’d like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
